Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well, I'm here because of the intercoms at work. I keep hearing these voices coming from the call-light speaker, and the hallway vents too, actually. They keep telling me exactly which patient records have been changed. It is as clear as somebody standing right next to me, even when the unit is dead quiet and nobody else hears a thing. Which, you know, the night shift is usually quiet. I've been a nursing assistant for a long time, and I always preferred nights. I took some college classes back in the day, thinking I'd do the RN program, but I just liked being hands-on with the patients, you know? Like Mrs. Higgins in room 4, she always wanted extra ice chips. I used to bring her the good crushed ice from the cafeteria downstairs. The cafeteria food is terrible now, though. They changed vendors last year and the meatloaf is just... it's practically rubber. I don't know how they expect people to heal eating that. But anyway, yeah, the voices from the vents are just so loud.

Well, it's not that I'm seeing things, it's just that I know what's going on with the wound-care charts. I've been doing this a long time, right? So I can see the pattern. Management doesn't want anyone to see it, but it's there. My supervisor, Brenda, she keeps telling me, "Oh, it's just late entries, it's just coding mistakes." But Brenda's always been like that. She's more worried about her vacation time. She went to Florida last month, actually. Brought back these little keychains for everyone. I don't even use keychains, I keep my keys on a lanyard. But anyway, I can tell they're watching what I write in those charts now. They know I'm the one who caught the pattern. I just have to be careful about what I document, because they're definitely keeping an eye on me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Sad? No, not really. I mean, I don't have time to be sad or sit around doing nothing. There's just too much going on, especially with those charts. I don't know how anyone could just lose interest when there are actual things happening right in front of them that need to be dealt with. Like, people are just so oblivious. My neighbor, Susan, she's exactly like that. She let her hydrangeas completely die this summer because she just couldn't be bothered to water them. And hydrangeas need water, it's right in the name! Hydro! I tried to tell her, I said, "Susan, you have to soak the roots," but she was too busy posting pictures of her cat on Facebook. It's a Persian cat, the ones with the really squished-in faces. I always feel bad for those cats, they look like they can't breathe properly. It's like those pugs, you know? They just snort all the time. But no, I don't get sad or hopeless. I'm too busy paying attention to what's actually going on.

Some days I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm meant to go in and just fix everything at work. And there is so much to fix. Like the ice machine on the third floor, it’s been leaking for weeks, and the maintenance guy, Gary, he just puts a rolled-up towel under it. A towel! Gary drives this massive truck, one of those lifted ones that takes up two parking spaces in the employee lot. I don't know why anyone needs a truck that big unless they're hauling horses. My grandfather had horses, beautiful Appaloosas. We used to brush them on the weekends when I was little, the coats were so soft. \n\nBut anyway, when I get in that zone and I'm getting it all done, if my husband or one of the other nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap at them. I completely go off, like they're blocking something really important. Because I am doing something important! No, not really. I mean, I don't have time to be sad or sit around doing nothing. There's just too much going on, especially with those charts. I don't know how anyone could just lose interest when there are actual things happening right in front of them that need to be dealt with. Like, people are just so oblivious. My neighbor, Susan, she's exactly like that. She let her hydrangeas completely die this summer because she just couldn't be bothered to water them. And hydrangeas need water, it's right in the name! Hydro! I tried to tell her, I said, \"Susan, you have to soak the roots,\" but she was too busy posting pictures of her cat on Facebook. It's a Persian cat, the ones with the really squished-in faces. I always feel bad for those cats, they look like they can't breathe properly. It's like those pugs, you know? They just snort all the time. But no, I don't get sad or hopeless. I'm too busy paying attention to what's actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or stopped enjoying things you used to like, even for a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, no. My grandmother used to make these elaborate jello molds with fruit suspended in them. She had this copper mold shaped like a fish, which always seemed so strange to me. I'm too busy for that.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or so unusually confident that it was noticeably different from your usual self, even if it didn't last very long?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 50 / nursing_assistant / some_college\n- severity: Severe\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Elevated_Irritable_Mood\nmanifestation: \"Some days I feel like I'm finally awake and meant to fix everything at work, but if my husband or a nurse tells me to slow down I snap at them like they're blocking something important.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, energy? Yes, definitely. Sometimes I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm the only one who can see what needs to be fixed at the clinic, and I just have this incredible drive to get it all done. But then, if my husband or one of the charge nurses tells me to slow down or take a break, I just... I snap at them. It's like they're standing in the way of something incredibly important and they just don't get it. 

They just don't get it at all. It's like trying to explain a new charting system to someone who still uses a flip phone. My aunt still has a flip phone, can you believe that? She refuses to get a smartphone because she thinks the government is tracking her through the apps. Which, I mean, maybe they are, but what do they care about a 70-year-old woman's grocery list? She buys the most bizarre things, too. Canned bread. Have you ever heard of canned bread? It's a New England thing, apparently, but it just sounds so unnatural. Bread shouldn't come out of a tin cylinder. It reminds me of those little Vienna sausages my dad used to eat when we went camping. Camping is just the worst. You sleep on the hard ground, you get eaten alive by mosquitoes, and for what? To be "one with nature"? Nature is dirty. I prefer a nice hotel with a high thread count on the sheets. But yes, the energy is definitely there, and I just can't stand it when people try to get in my way when I'm on a roll.

I don't get down like that. Two whole weeks of just moping around and not liking anything? Who has the time for that? I definitely still enjoy my hobbies and the things I like to do. \n\nI love to bake, for instance. I make these incredible lemon bars. We use this industrial floor cleaner at the clinic, it comes in these massive yellow jugs, and it smells just like fake lemons and bleach. My husband's brother went on a cruise to Alaska last year and said it was freezing the whole time. Why would you want to be on a giant boat right next to a bunch of icebergs? But no, I don't get depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time lasting at least two weeks where you experienced five or more of the following nearly every day: feeling sad, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, changes in appetite or weight, trouble sleeping or sleeping too much, feeling restless or slowed down, feeling worthless or guilty, or having trouble thinking or concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I just... I feel like we're going in circles here. I don't get sad or lose interest or sleep too much. If anything, I don't sleep enough, but that's because there's just too much to do. And my appetite is fine. I eat when I need to. Although, the cafeteria food at the hospital is just abysmal lately. They switched food service providers in January to save money, and now everything tastes like cardboard. Even the jello! How do you mess up jello? It's just sugar and water and that powder. He just leaves it on the grill and wanders off to check his phone. Phones are ruining everything, honestly. Nobody pays attention to anything right in front of them anymore. But no, I haven't had any two weeks of feeling worthless or whatever you said. It's like they're standing in the way of something incredibly important and they just don't get it. \n\nThey just don't get it at all. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, all the time. It's like... my mind just keeps jumping from wound measurements to patient initials to the dates on the forms, and then to what it all actually means. It's exactly like having ten tabs open on a computer at once and none of them will close, no matter how many times you click the little X. 

Speaking of the forms, they just changed the layout of our intake sheets at the clinic last month and made the boxes for the initials so unbelievably tiny. You practically need a magnifying glass to write in them. And they insist we use black ink only now. Blue ink is completely outlawed. My supervisor, Brenda, actually threw away a whole box of perfectly good blue click-pens. Just tossed them right in the trash! It’s so wasteful, and I just can't stand waste. 

When I was growing up, my mother used to make us wash and reuse aluminum foil. She’d rinse it in the sink and hang it over the faucet to dry. It always had these little crinkles in it so it never really wrapped right the second time around, especially on sandwiches. I took a turkey sandwich to school once wrapped in reused foil and the mayonnaise leaked everywhere. Mayonnaise is really hard to get out of a canvas backpack. I had to scrub it with baking soda for an hour. Baking soda is a miracle worker, really, you can use it for almost anything. You can even put an open box in the fridge to absorb smells. My husband put half an onion in the fridge without putting it in a container first, and the whole kitchen smelled like a deli for a week. I told him to put it in a Tupperware, but he never listens. But yes, my thoughts definitely do that, they just go and go and go.

Like Mrs. Higgins in room 4, she always wanted extra ice chips. I used to bring her the good crushed ice from the cafeteria downstairs. The cafeteria food is terrible now, though. They changed vendors last year and the meatloaf is just... it's practically rubber. I don't know how they expect people to heal eating that. But anyway, yeah, the voices from the vents are just so loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you believed something was real that others didn't seem to notice or agree with, like seeing something that wasn't there or feeling like you were being watched when you weren't?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not that I'm seeing things, it's just that I know what's going on with the wound-care charts. I've been doing this a long time, right? So I can see the pattern. Management doesn't want anyone to see it, but it's there. My supervisor, Brenda, she keeps telling me, \"Oh, it's just late entries, it's just coding mistakes.\" But Brenda's always been like that. Even when we had that whole issue with the linen cart being left in the fire exit, she just brushed it off. I told her, \"Brenda, the fire marshal is going to fine us,\" but she didn't care. She's more worried about her vacation time. She went to Florida last month, actually. Brought back these little keychains for everyone. I don't even use keychains, I keep my keys on a lanyard. But anyway, I can tell they're watching what I write in those charts now. They know I'm the one who caught the pattern. I just have to be careful about what I document, because they're definitely keeping an eye on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like certain people or groups are specifically targeting you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, not physical harm, I don't think, but they are definitely targeting my job. Like I was just saying, I absolutely know those wound-care charts are showing a pattern that management does not want anyone to see. Brenda can keep making excuses and saying it's just late entries or coding mistakes until she's blue in the face, but I can tell they're watching exactly what I write now because they know I'm the one who caught it. \n\nThey audit my notes all the time now. Notes are so important, you know. You always have to use black ink if it's on the backup paper forms. I only ever use my own pens, anyway. Pilot G2, the 0.7 millimeter ones. The 0.5 ones just scratch the paper, and I can't stand that scratchy feeling. It's exactly like nails on a chalkboard. We had a real chalkboard in my third-grade class, Mrs. Gable's room, and she would always make this horrible squeaking noise when she wrote the cursive letter Z. I never really saw the point of cursive anyway, especially now that everyone just types everything. My nephew types like a hundred words a minute, but he just plays those computer video games all night in his basement. He drinks too much of that Mountain Dew, it's going to rot his teeth out. I told my sister she needs to cut him off. But yeah, management is definitely keeping tabs on me because of what I found in those charts.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was very low or very high for several days in a row, where you felt unusually sad, hopeless, or lost interest in things, or on the flip side, unusually energetic, euphoric, or needed less sleep?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely on the high side. Some days I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm meant to go in and just fix everything at work. And there is so much to fix. Like the ice machine on the third floor, it’s been leaking for weeks, and the maintenance guy, Gary, he just puts a rolled-up towel under it. A towel! Gary drives this massive truck, one of those lifted ones that takes up two parking spaces in the employee lot. I don't know why anyone needs a truck that big unless they're hauling horses. My grandfather had horses, beautiful Appaloosas. We used to brush them on the weekends when I was little, the coats were so soft. \n\nBut anyway, when I get in that zone and I'm getting it all done, if my husband or one of the other nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap at them. I completely go off, like they're blocking something really important. Because I am doing something important! But my husband just doesn't get it, he just sits in his recliner watching the Golf Channel. Golf is so incredibly boring to watch on TV. It's just whispering and grass. Why do the announcers whisper so much? It's not like the golfers can hear them through the television.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually sad, hopeless, or lost interest in things for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, not really. I mean, I don't have time to be sad or sit around doing nothing. There's just too much going on, especially with those charts. I don't know how anyone could just lose interest when there are actual things happening right in front of them that need to be dealt with. Like, people are just so oblivious. My neighbor, Susan, she's exactly like that. She let her hydrangeas completely die this summer because she just couldn't be bothered to water them. And hydrangeas need water, it's right in the name! Hydro! I tried to tell her, I said, \"Susan, you have to soak the roots,\" but she was too busy posting pictures of her cat on Facebook. It's a Persian cat, the ones with the really squished-in faces. I always feel bad for those cats, they look like they can't breathe properly. It's like those pugs, you know? They just snort all the time. But no, I don't get sad or hopeless. I'm too busy paying attention to what's actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or stopped enjoying things you used to like, even for a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, no. I don't get down like that. Two whole weeks of just moping around and not liking anything? Who has the time for that? I definitely still enjoy my hobbies and the things I like to do. \n\nI love to bake, for instance. I make these incredible lemon bars. You have to use real Meyer lemons, though, not that awful bottled juice. The bottled stuff tastes exactly like floor cleaner. We use this industrial floor cleaner at the clinic, it comes in these massive yellow jugs, and it smells just like fake lemons and bleach. It gives me a terrible headache if the night crew uses too much of it. The night crew gets away with murder, honestly. They just sit at the nurses' station online shopping half the night. I caught one of the temps looking at bathing suits at two in the morning. Who buys a bathing suit in November? Unless you're going on a cruise, I guess. My husband's brother went on a cruise to Alaska last year and said it was freezing the whole time. Why would you want to be on a giant boat right next to a bunch of icebergs? We all know how that turned out for the Titanic. But no, I don't get depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time lasting at least two weeks where you experienced five or more of the following nearly every day: feeling sad, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, changes in appetite or weight, trouble sleeping or sleeping too much, feeling restless or slowed down, feeling worthless or guilty, or having trouble thinking or concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I just... I feel like we're going in circles here. I don't get sad or lose interest or sleep too much. If anything, I don't sleep enough, but that's because there's just too much to do. And my appetite is fine. I eat when I need to. Although, the cafeteria food at the hospital is just abysmal lately. They switched food service providers in January to save money, and now everything tastes like cardboard. Even the jello! How do you mess up jello? It's just sugar and water and that powder. My grandmother used to make these elaborate jello molds with fruit suspended in them. She had this copper mold shaped like a fish, which always seemed so strange to me. Why would you want your dessert to look like a fish? Fish are for dinner, not dessert. We had salmon last night, actually. My husband grilled it, but he always overcooks it until it's dry. He just leaves it on the grill and wanders off to check his phone. Phones are ruining everything, honestly. Nobody pays attention to anything right in front of them anymore. But no, I haven't had any two weeks of feeling worthless or whatever you said. I'm too busy for that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or so unusually confident that it was noticeably different from your usual self, even if it didn't last very long?\n\nPatient: Oh, energy? Yes, definitely. Sometimes I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm the only one who can see what needs to be fixed at the clinic, and I just have this incredible drive to get it all done. But then, if my husband or one of the charge nurses tells me to slow down or take a break, I just... I snap at them. It's like they're standing in the way of something incredibly important and they just don't get it. \n\nThey just don't get it at all. It's like trying to explain a new charting system to someone who still uses a flip phone. My aunt still has a flip phone, can you believe that? She refuses to get a smartphone because she thinks the government is tracking her through the apps. Which, I mean, maybe they are, but what do they care about a 70-year-old woman's grocery list? She buys the most bizarre things, too. Canned bread. Have you ever heard of canned bread? It's a New England thing, apparently, but it just sounds so unnatural. Bread shouldn't come out of a tin cylinder. It reminds me of those little Vienna sausages my dad used to eat when we went camping. Camping is just the worst. You sleep on the hard ground, you get eaten alive by mosquitoes, and for what? To be \"one with nature\"? Nature is dirty. I prefer a nice hotel with a high thread count on the sheets. But yes, the energy is definitely there, and I just can't stand it when people try to get in my way when I'm on a roll.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you couldn’t keep up, or when ideas just poured out of your head one after another?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's like... my mind just keeps jumping from wound measurements to patient initials to the dates on the forms, and then to what it all actually means. It's exactly like having ten tabs open on a computer at once and none of them will close, no matter how many times you click the little X. \n\nSpeaking of the forms, they just changed the layout of our intake sheets at the clinic last month and made the boxes for the initials so unbelievably tiny. You practically need a magnifying glass to write in them. And they insist we use black ink only now. Blue ink is completely outlawed. My supervisor, Brenda, actually threw away a whole box of perfectly good blue click-pens. Just tossed them right in the trash! It’s so wasteful, and I just can't stand waste. \n\nWhen I was growing up, my mother used to make us wash and reuse aluminum foil. She’d rinse it in the sink and hang it over the faucet to dry. It always had these little crinkles in it so it never really wrapped right the second time around, especially on sandwiches. I took a turkey sandwich to school once wrapped in reused foil and the mayonnaise leaked everywhere. Mayonnaise is really hard to get out of a canvas backpack. I had to scrub it with baking soda for an hour. Baking soda is a miracle worker, really, you can use it for almost anything. You can even put an open box in the fridge to absorb smells. My husband put half an onion in the fridge without putting it in a container first, and the whole kitchen smelled like a deli for a week. I told him to put it in a Tupperware, but he never listens. But yes, my thoughts definitely do that, they just go and go and go.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Directed at me? Well, yes, exactly. I know the wound-care charts are showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. It's so obvious to me. My supervisor keeps trying to brush it off, telling me it's just late entries or coding mistakes. Coding mistakes! Please. I can tell they're watching what I write now because I'm the one who actually caught it. They think I don't notice them monitoring my computer. 

Speaking of the computers, IT makes us change our passwords every thirty days, which is just exhausting. You have to use a capital letter, a number, and a special character, and you can't reuse the last ten passwords! My husband just writes his passwords on a yellow sticky note and sticks it right to his monitor at home. I tell him, "Anyone can see that!" but he says nobody goes in his home office anyway. Which is true, because his office is a complete disaster zone. He has stacks of old magazines from the 90s in there. *National Geographic*, mostly. I don't know why anyone would keep a magazine from 1998. The world has changed completely since then! The maps aren't even accurate anymore. Whole countries have completely different names now. 

It’s like when they decided Pluto wasn't a planet anymore. Just demoted it to a dwarf planet. It just seems so unfair to change the rules on Pluto after all this time. I remember making little styrofoam solar systems in the fourth grade and Pluto was right there on the end of the wire, painted purple. I used grape Kool-Aid powder to dye the paint because we ran out of purple watercolors. Kool-Aid stains everything, though. You get that on your fingers and you look like you've been crushing berries for a week. But anyway, yes, management is absolutely watching my chart entries because they know I see the pattern they're trying to hide.

It's not like the golfers can hear them through the television.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually sad, hopeless, or lost interest in things for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, not really. I mean, I don't have time to be sad or sit around doing nothing. There's just too much going on, especially with those charts. I don't know how anyone could just lose interest when there are actual things happening right in front of them that need to be dealt with. Like, people are just so oblivious. My neighbor, Susan, she's exactly like that. She let her hydrangeas completely die this summer because she just couldn't be bothered to water them. And hydrangeas need water, it's right in the name! Hydro! I tried to tell her, I said, \"Susan, you have to soak the roots,\" but she was too busy posting pictures of her cat on Facebook. It's a Persian cat, the ones with the really squished-in faces. I always feel bad for those cats, they look like they can't breathe properly. It's like those pugs, you know? They just snort all the time. But no, I don't get sad or hopeless. I'm too busy paying attention to what's actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or stopped enjoying things you used to like, even for a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, no. I don't get down like that. Two whole weeks of just moping around and not liking anything? Who has the time for that? I definitely still enjoy my hobbies and the things I like to do. \n\nI love to bake, for instance. I make these incredible lemon bars. You have to use real Meyer lemons, though, not that awful bottled juice. The bottled stuff tastes exactly like floor cleaner. We use this industrial floor cleaner at the clinic, it comes in these massive yellow jugs, and it smells just like fake lemons and bleach. It gives me a terrible headache if the night crew uses too much of it. The night crew gets away with murder, honestly. They just sit at the nurses' station online shopping half the night. I caught one of the temps looking at bathing suits at two in the morning. Who buys a bathing suit in November? Unless you're going on a cruise, I guess. My husband's brother went on a cruise to Alaska last year and said it was freezing the whole time. Why would you want to be on a giant boat right next to a bunch of icebergs? We all know how that turned out for the Titanic. But no, I don't get depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time lasting at least two weeks where you experienced five or more of the following nearly every day: feeling sad, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, changes in appetite or weight, trouble sleeping or sleeping too much, feeling restless or slowed down, feeling worthless or guilty, or having trouble thinking or concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I just... I feel like we're going in circles here. I don't get sad or lose interest or sleep too much. If anything, I don't sleep enough, but that's because there's just too much to do. And my appetite is fine. I eat when I need to. Although, the cafeteria food at the hospital is just abysmal lately. They switched food service providers in January to save money, and now everything tastes like cardboard. Even the jello! How do you mess up jello? It's just sugar and water and that powder. My grandmother used to make these elaborate jello molds with fruit suspended in them. She had this copper mold shaped like a fish, which always seemed so strange to me. Why would you want your dessert to look like a fish? Fish are for dinner, not dessert. We had salmon last night, actually. My husband grilled it, but he always overcooks it until it's dry. He just leaves it on the grill and wanders off to check his phone. Phones are ruining everything, honestly. Nobody pays attention to anything right in front of them anymore. But no, I haven't had any two weeks of feeling worthless or whatever you said. I'm too busy for that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or so unusually confident that it was noticeably different from your usual self, even if it didn't last very long?\n\nPatient: Oh, energy? Yes, definitely. Sometimes I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm the only one who can see what needs to be fixed at the clinic, and I just have this incredible drive to get it all done. But then, if my husband or one of the charge nurses tells me to slow down or take a break, I just... I snap at them. It's like they're standing in the way of something incredibly important and they just don't get it. \n\nThey just don't get it at all. It's like trying to explain a new charting system to someone who still uses a flip phone. My aunt still has a flip phone, can you believe that? She refuses to get a smartphone because she thinks the government is tracking her through the apps. Which, I mean, maybe they are, but what do they care about a 70-year-old woman's grocery list? She buys the most bizarre things, too. Canned bread. Have you ever heard of canned bread? It's a New England thing, apparently, but it just sounds so unnatural. Bread shouldn't come out of a tin cylinder. It reminds me of those little Vienna sausages my dad used to eat when we went camping. Camping is just the worst. You sleep on the hard ground, you get eaten alive by mosquitoes, and for what? To be \"one with nature\"? Nature is dirty. I prefer a nice hotel with a high thread count on the sheets. But yes, the energy is definitely there, and I just can't stand it when people try to get in my way when I'm on a roll.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you couldn’t keep up, or when ideas just poured out of your head one after another?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's like... my mind just keeps jumping from wound measurements to patient initials to the dates on the forms, and then to what it all actually means. I took a turkey sandwich to school once wrapped in reused foil and the mayonnaise leaked everywhere. Mayonnaise is really hard to get out of a canvas backpack. I had to scrub it with baking soda for an hour. Baking soda is a miracle worker, really, you can use it for almost anything. You can even put an open box in the fridge to absorb smells. My husband put half an onion in the fridge without putting it in a container first, and the whole kitchen smelled like a deli for a week. I told him to put it in a Tupperware, but he never listens. But yes, my thoughts definitely do that, they just go and go and go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being placed in your mind that aren't your own, or that events or messages in your environment are specifically directed at you, like something is meant just for you?\n\nPatient: Directed at me? Well, yes, like I was saying before, it's the charts. The wound-care charts. I know they're showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. I always thought that was so strange, keeping medicine with the butter. But he loved butter. He used to put it on everything, even saltine crackers. Have you ever tried that? It's surprisingly good, especially if you have a little bowl of chili to dip it in. Chili is perfect for this time of year, when it starts getting chilly outside. No pun intended! But yes, they are definitely watching what I write in those charts because they know I'm onto them.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "/no_think\nYou are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF25.0, F25.1, F25.2, F25.8\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because of the intercoms at work. I keep hearing these voices coming from the call-light speaker, and the hallway vents too, actually. They keep telling me exactly which patient records have been changed. It is as clear as somebody standing right next to me, even when the unit is dead quiet and nobody else hears a thing. Which, you know, the night shift is usually quiet. I've been a nursing assistant for a long time, and I always preferred nights. I took some college classes back in the day, thinking I'd do the RN program, but I just liked being hands-on with the patients, you know? Like Mrs. Higgins in room 4, she always wanted extra ice chips. I used to bring her the good crushed ice from the cafeteria downstairs. The cafeteria food is terrible now, though. They changed vendors last year and the meatloaf is just... it's practically rubber. I don't know how they expect people to heal eating that. But anyway, yeah, the voices from the vents are just so loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you believed something was real that others didn't seem to notice or agree with, like seeing something that wasn't there or feeling like you were being watched when you weren't?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not that I'm seeing things, it's just that I know what's going on with the wound-care charts. I've been doing this a long time, right? So I can see the pattern. Management doesn't want anyone to see it, but it's there. My supervisor, Brenda, she keeps telling me, \"Oh, it's just late entries, it's just coding mistakes.\" But Brenda's always been like that. Even when we had that whole issue with the linen cart being left in the fire exit, she just brushed it off. I told her, \"Brenda, the fire marshal is going to fine us,\" but she didn't care. She's more worried about her vacation time. She went to Florida last month, actually. Brought back these little keychains for everyone. I don't even use keychains, I keep my keys on a lanyard. But anyway, I can tell they're watching what I write in those charts now. They know I'm the one who caught the pattern. I just have to be careful about what I document, because they're definitely keeping an eye on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like certain people or groups are specifically targeting you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, not physical harm, I don't think, but they are definitely targeting my job. Like I was just saying, I absolutely know those wound-care charts are showing a pattern that management does not want anyone to see. Brenda can keep making excuses and saying it's just late entries or coding mistakes until she's blue in the face, but I can tell they're watching exactly what I write now because they know I'm the one who caught it. \n\nThey audit my notes all the time now. Notes are so important, you know. You always have to use black ink if it's on the backup paper forms. I only ever use my own pens, anyway. Pilot G2, the 0.7 millimeter ones. The 0.5 ones just scratch the paper, and I can't stand that scratchy feeling. It's exactly like nails on a chalkboard. We had a real chalkboard in my third-grade class, Mrs. Gable's room, and she would always make this horrible squeaking noise when she wrote the cursive letter Z. I never really saw the point of cursive anyway, especially now that everyone just types everything. My nephew types like a hundred words a minute, but he just plays those computer video games all night in his basement. He drinks too much of that Mountain Dew, it's going to rot his teeth out. I told my sister she needs to cut him off. But yeah, management is definitely keeping tabs on me because of what I found in those charts.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was very low or very high for several days in a row, where you felt unusually sad, hopeless, or lost interest in things, or on the flip side, unusually energetic, euphoric, or needed less sleep?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely on the high side. Some days I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm meant to go in and just fix everything at work. And there is so much to fix. Like the ice machine on the third floor, it’s been leaking for weeks, and the maintenance guy, Gary, he just puts a rolled-up towel under it. A towel! Gary drives this massive truck, one of those lifted ones that takes up two parking spaces in the employee lot. I don't know why anyone needs a truck that big unless they're hauling horses. My grandfather had horses, beautiful Appaloosas. We used to brush them on the weekends when I was little, the coats were so soft. \n\nBut anyway, when I get in that zone and I'm getting it all done, if my husband or one of the other nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap at them. I completely go off, like they're blocking something really important. Because I am doing something important! But my husband just doesn't get it, he just sits in his recliner watching the Golf Channel. Golf is so incredibly boring to watch on TV. It's just whispering and grass. Why do the announcers whisper so much? It's not like the golfers can hear them through the television.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually sad, hopeless, or lost interest in things for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, not really. I mean, I don't have time to be sad or sit around doing nothing. There's just too much going on, especially with those charts. I don't know how anyone could just lose interest when there are actual things happening right in front of them that need to be dealt with. Like, people are just so oblivious. My neighbor, Susan, she's exactly like that. She let her hydrangeas completely die this summer because she just couldn't be bothered to water them. And hydrangeas need water, it's right in the name! Hydro! I tried to tell her, I said, \"Susan, you have to soak the roots,\" but she was too busy posting pictures of her cat on Facebook. It's a Persian cat, the ones with the really squished-in faces. I always feel bad for those cats, they look like they can't breathe properly. It's like those pugs, you know? They just snort all the time. But no, I don't get sad or hopeless. I'm too busy paying attention to what's actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or stopped enjoying things you used to like, even for a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, no. I don't get down like that. Two whole weeks of just moping around and not liking anything? Who has the time for that? I definitely still enjoy my hobbies and the things I like to do. \n\nI love to bake, for instance. I make these incredible lemon bars. You have to use real Meyer lemons, though, not that awful bottled juice. The bottled stuff tastes exactly like floor cleaner. We use this industrial floor cleaner at the clinic, it comes in these massive yellow jugs, and it smells just like fake lemons and bleach. It gives me a terrible headache if the night crew uses too much of it. The night crew gets away with murder, honestly. They just sit at the nurses' station online shopping half the night. I caught one of the temps looking at bathing suits at two in the morning. Who buys a bathing suit in November? Unless you're going on a cruise, I guess. My husband's brother went on a cruise to Alaska last year and said it was freezing the whole time. Why would you want to be on a giant boat right next to a bunch of icebergs? We all know how that turned out for the Titanic. But no, I don't get depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time lasting at least two weeks where you experienced five or more of the following nearly every day: feeling sad, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, changes in appetite or weight, trouble sleeping or sleeping too much, feeling restless or slowed down, feeling worthless or guilty, or having trouble thinking or concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I just... I feel like we're going in circles here. I don't get sad or lose interest or sleep too much. If anything, I don't sleep enough, but that's because there's just too much to do. And my appetite is fine. I eat when I need to. Although, the cafeteria food at the hospital is just abysmal lately. They switched food service providers in January to save money, and now everything tastes like cardboard. Even the jello! How do you mess up jello? It's just sugar and water and that powder. My grandmother used to make these elaborate jello molds with fruit suspended in them. She had this copper mold shaped like a fish, which always seemed so strange to me. Why would you want your dessert to look like a fish? Fish are for dinner, not dessert. We had salmon last night, actually. My husband grilled it, but he always overcooks it until it's dry. He just leaves it on the grill and wanders off to check his phone. Phones are ruining everything, honestly. Nobody pays attention to anything right in front of them anymore. But no, I haven't had any two weeks of feeling worthless or whatever you said. I'm too busy for that.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a time when you felt so full of energy or so unusually confident that it was noticeably different from your usual self, even if it didn't last very long?\n\nPatient: Oh, energy? Yes, definitely. Sometimes I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm the only one who can see what needs to be fixed at the clinic, and I just have this incredible drive to get it all done. But then, if my husband or one of the charge nurses tells me to slow down or take a break, I just... I snap at them. It's like they're standing in the way of something incredibly important and they just don't get it. \n\nThey just don't get it at all. It's like trying to explain a new charting system to someone who still uses a flip phone. My aunt still has a flip phone, can you believe that? She refuses to get a smartphone because she thinks the government is tracking her through the apps. Which, I mean, maybe they are, but what do they care about a 70-year-old woman's grocery list? She buys the most bizarre things, too. Canned bread. Have you ever heard of canned bread? It's a New England thing, apparently, but it just sounds so unnatural. Bread shouldn't come out of a tin cylinder. It reminds me of those little Vienna sausages my dad used to eat when we went camping. Camping is just the worst. You sleep on the hard ground, you get eaten alive by mosquitoes, and for what? To be \"one with nature\"? Nature is dirty. I prefer a nice hotel with a high thread count on the sheets. But yes, the energy is definitely there, and I just can't stand it when people try to get in my way when I'm on a roll.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you couldn’t keep up, or when ideas just poured out of your head one after another?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's like... my mind just keeps jumping from wound measurements to patient initials to the dates on the forms, and then to what it all actually means. It's exactly like having ten tabs open on a computer at once and none of them will close, no matter how many times you click the little X. \n\nSpeaking of the forms, they just changed the layout of our intake sheets at the clinic last month and made the boxes for the initials so unbelievably tiny. You practically need a magnifying glass to write in them. And they insist we use black ink only now. Blue ink is completely outlawed. My supervisor, Brenda, actually threw away a whole box of perfectly good blue click-pens. Just tossed them right in the trash! It’s so wasteful, and I just can't stand waste. \n\nWhen I was growing up, my mother used to make us wash and reuse aluminum foil. She’d rinse it in the sink and hang it over the faucet to dry. It always had these little crinkles in it so it never really wrapped right the second time around, especially on sandwiches. I took a turkey sandwich to school once wrapped in reused foil and the mayonnaise leaked everywhere. Mayonnaise is really hard to get out of a canvas backpack. I had to scrub it with baking soda for an hour. Baking soda is a miracle worker, really, you can use it for almost anything. You can even put an open box in the fridge to absorb smells. My husband put half an onion in the fridge without putting it in a container first, and the whole kitchen smelled like a deli for a week. I told him to put it in a Tupperware, but he never listens. But yes, my thoughts definitely do that, they just go and go and go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being placed in your mind that aren't your own, or that events or messages in your environment are specifically directed at you, like something is meant just for you?\n\nPatient: Directed at me? Well, yes, exactly. I know the wound-care charts are showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. It's so obvious to me. My supervisor keeps trying to brush it off, telling me it's just late entries or coding mistakes. Coding mistakes! Please. I can tell they're watching what I write now because I'm the one who actually caught it. They think I don't notice them monitoring my computer. \n\nSpeaking of the computers, IT makes us change our passwords every thirty days, which is just exhausting. You have to use a capital letter, a number, and a special character, and you can't reuse the last ten passwords! My husband just writes his passwords on a yellow sticky note and sticks it right to his monitor at home. I tell him, \"Anyone can see that!\" but he says nobody goes in his home office anyway. Which is true, because his office is a complete disaster zone. He has stacks of old magazines from the 90s in there. *National Geographic*, mostly. I don't know why anyone would keep a magazine from 1998. The world has changed completely since then! The maps aren't even accurate anymore. Whole countries have completely different names now. \n\nIt’s like when they decided Pluto wasn't a planet anymore. Just demoted it to a dwarf planet. It just seems so unfair to change the rules on Pluto after all this time. I remember making little styrofoam solar systems in the fourth grade and Pluto was right there on the end of the wire, painted purple. I used grape Kool-Aid powder to dye the paint because we ran out of purple watercolors. Kool-Aid stains everything, though. You get that on your fingers and you look like you've been crushing berries for a week. But anyway, yes, management is absolutely watching my chart entries because they know I see the pattern they're trying to hide.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being put into your mind that aren't your own, or like things happening around you—like something on TV, in the newspaper, or something someone said—were meant specifically for you, even if others didn't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was saying before, it's the charts. The wound-care charts. I know they're showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. My supervisor, Brenda, she keeps trying to brush it off, telling me it's just late entries or coding mistakes. But I know better. I can tell they're watching what I write now because I'm the one who actually caught it. They're definitely monitoring me. \n\nIt's just like how they monitor the temperatures in the medication refrigerators. They have these little digital thermometers that beep if it gets even one degree too warm. It beeped for an entire hour last Tuesday because someone didn't shut the door all the way after getting out the insulin. Insulin is so expensive, you know. My uncle had to take it, and he used to complain about the cost all the time. He kept his in the butter compartment of his fridge, right next to the Country Crock. I always thought that was so strange, keeping medicine with the butter. But he loved butter. He used to put it on everything, even saltine crackers. Have you ever tried that? It's surprisingly good, especially if you have a little bowl of chili to dip it in. Chili is perfect for this time of year, when it starts getting chilly outside. No pun intended! But yes, they are definitely watching what I write in those charts because they know I'm onto them.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. My husband put half an onion in the fridge without putting it in a container first, and the whole kitchen smelled like a deli for a week. I told him to put it in a Tupperware, but he never listens. But yes, my thoughts definitely do that, they just go and go and go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being placed in your mind that aren't your own, or that events or messages in your environment are specifically directed at you, like something is meant just for you?\n\nPatient: Directed at me? Well, yes, exactly. I know the wound-care charts are showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. It's so obvious to me. My supervisor keeps trying to brush it off, telling me it's just late entries or coding mistakes. Coding mistakes! Please. I can tell they're watching what I write now because I'm the one who actually caught it. They think I don't notice them monitoring my computer. \n\nSpeaking of the computers, IT makes us change our passwords every thirty days, which is just exhausting. You have to use a capital letter, a number, and a special character, and you can't reuse the last ten passwords! My husband just writes his passwords on a yellow sticky note and sticks it right to his monitor at home. I tell him, \"Anyone can see that!\" but he says nobody goes in his home office anyway. Which is true, because his office is a complete disaster zone. He has stacks of old magazines from the 90s in there. *National Geographic*, mostly. I don't know why anyone would keep a magazine from 1998. The world has changed completely since then! The maps aren't even accurate anymore. Whole countries have completely different names now. \n\nIt’s like when they decided Pluto wasn't a planet anymore. Just demoted it to a dwarf planet. It just seems so unfair to change the rules on Pluto after all this time. I remember making little styrofoam solar systems in the fourth grade and Pluto was right there on the end of the wire, painted purple. I used grape Kool-Aid powder to dye the paint because we ran out of purple watercolors. Kool-Aid stains everything, though. You get that on your fingers and you look like you've been crushing berries for a week. But anyway, yes, management is absolutely watching my chart entries because they know I see the pattern they're trying to hide.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being put into your mind that aren't your own, or like things happening around you—like something on TV, in the newspaper, or something someone said—were meant specifically for you, even if others didn't see it that way?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, like I was saying before, it's the charts. It beeped for an entire hour last Tuesday because someone didn't shut the door all the way after getting out the insulin. Insulin is so expensive, you know. I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again, Doctor. Like I just told you, it's the wound-care charts. I hate when that happens. It reminds me of when you make chocolate milk with the syrup and you don't stir it enough, so you just get a giant mouthful of chocolate sludge at the very end. 

My kids used to do that when they were little. They’d use half the bottle of Hershey's syrup for one tiny glass of milk. Kids have absolutely no concept of moderation. They just want everything sweet, sweet, sweet. I tried buying that sugar-free syrup once, the kind made with Splenda, but they knew immediately. They wouldn't touch it. I ended up pouring it over ice cream for myself so it wouldn't go to waste, but it had this terrible metallic aftertaste. I don't know why they even sell things that taste like metal. I always thought that was so strange, keeping medicine with the butter. But he loved butter. He used to put it on everything, even saltine crackers. Have you ever tried that? It's surprisingly good, especially if you have a little bowl of chili to dip it in. Chili is perfect for this time of year, when it starts getting chilly outside. No pun intended! But yes, they are definitely watching what I write in those charts because they know I'm onto them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being placed in your mind that aren't your own, or like things happening around you—like something on TV, in the newspaper, or something someone said—were meant specifically for you, even if others didn't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again, Doctor. Brenda keeps giving me these ridiculous excuses about late entries and coding mistakes, but I'm not stupid. I know they're watching everything I write now because I'm the one who caught them. \n\nWatching... just like the new cameras they put up in the breakroom. They claim it's to stop people from stealing lunches, which, to be fair, is an actual problem on our floor. Someone took my yogurt last Thursday. It was a peach yogurt, the kind with the fruit on the bottom. You have to stir it really, really well or all the fruit stays stuck down there. I hate when that happens. It reminds me of when you make chocolate milk with the syrup and you don't stir it enough, so you just get a giant mouthful of chocolate sludge at the very end. \n\nMy kids used to do that when they were little. They’d use half the bottle of Hershey's syrup for one tiny glass of milk. Kids have absolutely no concept of moderation. They just want everything sweet, sweet, sweet. I tried buying that sugar-free syrup once, the kind made with Splenda, but they knew immediately. They wouldn't touch it. I ended up pouring it over ice cream for myself so it wouldn't go to waste, but it had this terrible metallic aftertaste. I don't know why they even sell things that taste like metal. But yes, I know for a fact they're monitoring my entries because I see what they're trying to hide.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Doctor, are you even listening to me? We keep going in circles here. I don't care about the TV or the radio or what strangers are doing. I've told you three times now, the pattern is in the wound-care charts! It's management. They are hiding it, and even though my supervisor keeps brushing it off as late entries or coding mistakes, I know they are watching everything I write because I'm the one who actually caught it! 

They watch me like a hawk. Which is funny, because they don't watch anything else. Take Gary, the security guard down in our lobby. He doesn't watch a thing. He just sits at that front desk doing those jumbo crossword puzzles all day long. I don't even know why they pay him. And he uses a pen, too, which I just think is incredibly arrogant. Who does a crossword puzzle in pen? 

My father used to do that, but he was an English teacher, so I guess he earned the right. He taught high school for thirty-five years. He used to make my brother and me diagram sentences at the dinner table. Can you imagine? Trying to eat your meatloaf while figuring out what a dangling participle is. Meatloaf is one of those things, though... it's either really good or it's just a dry brick of hamburger. You have to soak the breadcrumbs in a little bit of milk first to keep it moist. And you need a really good glaze on top! Just ketchup, brown sugar, and a splash of apple cider vinegar. The vinegar gives it that little tang. I tried making it with turkey once because it's supposed to be healthier, but it was just awful. Completely dried out. But anyway, yes, management is absolutely watching my every move because of what I found in those charts.

Are you serious right now? We are going over the exact same thing again! I am not talking about the TV or the radio. I don't care about the radio. It's the charts! The wound-care charts! I've told you this four times now. I know they are showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. My supervisor, Brenda, she keeps giving me the same tired excuses about late entries and coding mistakes, but I can tell they are watching what I write because I'm the one who caught it. 

They are watching me, just like... well, like the way the state inspectors watch us when they come in for their annual survey. They just stand there with their little clipboards, hovering over you while you're trying to do a simple bed bath. It's so nerve-wracking. You forget how to even hold a washcloth when someone is staring at you like that. Washcloths at the hospital are terrible anyway. They're so thin and scratchy, practically like sandpaper. You can't even get a good lather going with the soap. 

I buy those nice, thick Egyptian cotton ones for my house. They cost a little more, but they are so worth it. You have to wash them a few times before they really get absorbent, though. And never use fabric softener on towels! It coats the fibers and ruins them. My sister-in-law uses fabric softener on everything, and her towels feel like they're covered in wax. You try to dry your hands and the water just smears around. She uses that lavender-scented one, too, which gives me a terrible headache. Lavender is supposed to be relaxing, but to me, it just smells like old potpourri in a dusty antique shop. I prefer citrus scents. Lemon or grapefruit. Grapefruit is so refreshing in the morning. But anyway, I know they are monitoring my charting because I found the pattern they're hiding.

Is there a hidden camera in here? Are you literally reading from a script? We have been over this, Doctor! I don't care about the radio or the TV. I am talking about the wound-care charts! Management is hiding a pattern, and Brenda can spin it all she wants, saying it's just coding mistakes or late entries, but I'm the one who caught it, and I know they are watching every single word I write now. 

They think I'm just going to let it go, but I don't let things go. Just like I didn't let it go when my neighbor's dog kept digging up my hydrangeas. She tried to tell me it was raccoons. Raccoons! Raccoons don't leave giant paw prints in the mud. They get into the garbage. We have to use those heavy-duty bungee cords on our trash cans now to keep them out. The thick black ones with the metal hooks on the ends. You have to be so careful with those hooks, though. My uncle Joey snapped one back into his eye when he was tying down a mattress on his pickup truck. He had to wear an eye patch for six weeks. He looked exactly like a pirate. He actually dressed up as a pirate for Halloween that year, which was pretty clever of him to make the most of it. Halloween used to be so much fun when we made the kids' costumes from scratch. Now you go to the store and it's just cheap polyester garbage in a plastic bag that costs fifty dollars. Fifty dollars! You could buy a whole week's worth of groceries for that back in the day. Groceries are just outrageous now anyway. A carton of eggs is practically a luxury item. But no, no secret messages from the TV. It's the charts, and they are watching me because of what I know.

I used grape Kool-Aid powder to dye the paint because we ran out of purple watercolors. Kool-Aid stains everything, though. The wound-care charts. I know they're showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. My supervisor, Brenda, she keeps trying to brush it off, telling me it's just late entries or coding mistakes. But I know better. I can tell they're watching what I write now because I'm the one who actually caught it. They're definitely monitoring me. \n\nIt's just like how they monitor the temperatures in the medication refrigerators. It beeped for an entire hour last Tuesday because someone didn't shut the door all the way after getting out the insulin. Insulin is so expensive, you know. You have to be so careful with those hooks, though. My uncle Joey snapped one back into his eye when he was tying down a mattress on his pickup truck. He had to wear an eye patch for six weeks. Are you... is this a joke? Am I being pranked right now? Doctor, you just asked me the exact same question! I feel like I'm talking to a brick wall. 

No! Nobody is talking to me through the television! It is the wound-care charts! The charts! How many times do I have to say it? I know they're showing a pattern that management desperately doesn't want anyone to see. My supervisor, Brenda, keeps trying to brush it off, telling me it's just late entries or coding mistakes, but I'm not stupid. I can tell they are watching everything I write. They are watching me because I'm the one who actually caught it.

You repeating yourself like this... it's exactly like my patient in room 412, Mr. Abernathy. He asks me what time breakfast is every ten minutes. It's heartbreaking, really. He used to be a baker. Made the most incredible sourdough, supposedly. Sourdough is so tricky because you have to keep the starter alive. It's basically like having a pet. You have to feed it flour and water every single day. My neighbor tried to give me some of her starter once in this little glass jar, but it smelled like old gym socks, so I threw it right in the trash. 

I just cannot stand bad smells in the kitchen. I always boil a pot of water with cinnamon sticks and orange peels after I cook fish. Salmon is the worst for that. The smell lingers in the curtains for days. But salmon is full of omega-3s, which are supposed to be good for your brain. Maybe you should eat some salmon, Doctor, because your memory is clearly failing if you can't remember that I just told you—for the fifth time—that it's the charts, not the radio!

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Well, I'm here because of the intercoms at work. I keep hearing these voices coming from the call-light speaker, and the hallway vents too, actually. They keep telling me exactly which patient records have been changed. It is as clear as somebody standing right next to me, even when the unit is dead quiet and nobody else hears a thing. Which, you know, the night shift is usually quiet. I've been a nursing assistant for a long time, and I always preferred nights. I took some college classes back in the day, thinking I'd do the RN program, but I just liked being hands-on with the patients, you know? Like Mrs. Higgins in room 4, she always wanted extra ice chips. I used to bring her the good crushed ice from the cafeteria downstairs. The cafeteria food is terrible now, though. They changed vendors last year and the meatloaf is just... it's practically rubber. I don't know how they expect people to heal eating that. But anyway, yeah, the voices from the vents are just so loud.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had experiences where you believed something was real that others didn't seem to notice or agree with, like seeing something that wasn't there or feeling like you were being watched when you weren't?\n\nPatient: Well, it's not that I'm seeing things, it's just that I know what's going on with the wound-care charts. I've been doing this a long time, right? So I can see the pattern. Management doesn't want anyone to see it, but it's there. My supervisor, Brenda, she keeps telling me, \"Oh, it's just late entries, it's just coding mistakes.\" But Brenda's always been like that. Even when we had that whole issue with the linen cart being left in the fire exit, she just brushed it off. I told her, \"Brenda, the fire marshal is going to fine us,\" but she didn't care. She's more worried about her vacation time. She went to Florida last month, actually. Brought back these little keychains for everyone. I don't even use keychains, I keep my keys on a lanyard. But anyway, I can tell they're watching what I write in those charts now. They know I'm the one who caught the pattern. I just have to be careful about what I document, because they're definitely keeping an eye on me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like certain people or groups are specifically targeting you or trying to harm you in some way?\n\nPatient: Well, not physical harm, I don't think, but they are definitely targeting my job. Like I was just saying, I absolutely know those wound-care charts are showing a pattern that management does not want anyone to see. Brenda can keep making excuses and saying it's just late entries or coding mistakes until she's blue in the face, but I can tell they're watching exactly what I write now because they know I'm the one who caught it. \n\nThey audit my notes all the time now. Notes are so important, you know. You always have to use black ink if it's on the backup paper forms. I only ever use my own pens, anyway. Pilot G2, the 0.7 millimeter ones. The 0.5 ones just scratch the paper, and I can't stand that scratchy feeling. It's exactly like nails on a chalkboard. We had a real chalkboard in my third-grade class, Mrs. Gable's room, and she would always make this horrible squeaking noise when she wrote the cursive letter Z. I never really saw the point of cursive anyway, especially now that everyone just types everything. My nephew types like a hundred words a minute, but he just plays those computer video games all night in his basement. He drinks too much of that Mountain Dew, it's going to rot his teeth out. I told my sister she needs to cut him off. But yeah, management is definitely keeping tabs on me because of what I found in those charts.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your mood was very low or very high for several days in a row, where you felt unusually sad, hopeless, or lost interest in things, or on the flip side, unusually energetic, euphoric, or needed less sleep?\n\nPatient: Oh, definitely on the high side. Some days I feel like I'm finally, truly awake, you know? Like I'm meant to go in and just fix everything at work. And there is so much to fix. Like the ice machine on the third floor, it’s been leaking for weeks, and the maintenance guy, Gary, he just puts a rolled-up towel under it. A towel! Gary drives this massive truck, one of those lifted ones that takes up two parking spaces in the employee lot. I don't know why anyone needs a truck that big unless they're hauling horses. My grandfather had horses, beautiful Appaloosas. We used to brush them on the weekends when I was little, the coats were so soft. \n\nBut anyway, when I get in that zone and I'm getting it all done, if my husband or one of the other nurses tells me to slow down, I just snap at them. I completely go off, like they're blocking something really important. Because I am doing something important! But my husband just doesn't get it, he just sits in his recliner watching the Golf Channel. Golf is so incredibly boring to watch on TV. It's just whispering and grass. Why do the announcers whisper so much? It's not like the golfers can hear them through the television.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when you felt unusually sad, hopeless, or lost interest in things for several days in a row?\n\nPatient: Sad? No, not really. I mean, I don't have time to be sad or sit around doing nothing. There's just too much going on, especially with those charts. I don't know how anyone could just lose interest when there are actual things happening right in front of them that need to be dealt with. Like, people are just so oblivious. My neighbor, Susan, she's exactly like that. She let her hydrangeas completely die this summer because she just couldn't be bothered to water them. And hydrangeas need water, it's right in the name! Hydro! I tried to tell her, I said, \"Susan, you have to soak the roots,\" but she was too busy posting pictures of her cat on Facebook. It's a Persian cat, the ones with the really squished-in faces. I always feel bad for those cats, they look like they can't breathe properly. It's like those pugs, you know? They just snort all the time. But no, I don't get sad or hopeless. I'm too busy paying attention to what's actually going on.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time when you felt down or hopeless most of the day, nearly every day, or stopped enjoying things you used to like, even for a couple of weeks?\n\nPatient: Like I was just saying, no. I don't get down like that. Two whole weeks of just moping around and not liking anything? Who has the time for that? I definitely still enjoy my hobbies and the things I like to do. \n\nI love to bake, for instance. I make these incredible lemon bars. You have to use real Meyer lemons, though, not that awful bottled juice. The bottled stuff tastes exactly like floor cleaner. We use this industrial floor cleaner at the clinic, it comes in these massive yellow jugs, and it smells just like fake lemons and bleach. My husband's brother went on a cruise to Alaska last year and said it was freezing the whole time. Why would you want to be on a giant boat right next to a bunch of icebergs? We all know how that turned out for the Titanic. But no, I don't get depressed.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a period of time lasting at least two weeks where you experienced five or more of the following nearly every day: feeling sad, losing interest in things you usually enjoy, changes in appetite or weight, trouble sleeping or sleeping too much, feeling restless or slowed down, feeling worthless or guilty, or having trouble thinking or concentrating?\n\nPatient: No, I just... I feel like we're going in circles here. I don't get sad or lose interest or sleep too much. If anything, I don't sleep enough, but that's because there's just too much to do. And my appetite is fine. I eat when I need to. Although, the cafeteria food at the hospital is just abysmal lately. They switched food service providers in January to save money, and now everything tastes like cardboard. Even the jello! How do you mess up jello? It's just sugar and water and that powder. It's like they're standing in the way of something incredibly important and they just don't get it. \n\nThey just don't get it at all. But yes, the energy is definitely there, and I just can't stand it when people try to get in my way when I'm on a roll.\n\nDoctor: Have there been times when your thoughts were racing so fast that you couldn’t keep up, or when ideas just poured out of your head one after another?\n\nPatient: Yes, all the time. It's like... my mind just keeps jumping from wound measurements to patient initials to the dates on the forms, and then to what it all actually means. You practically need a magnifying glass to write in them. And they insist we use black ink only now. Blue ink is completely outlawed. My supervisor, Brenda, actually threw away a whole box of perfectly good blue click-pens. Just tossed them right in the trash! It’s so wasteful, and I just can't stand waste. \n\nWhen I was growing up, my mother used to make us wash and reuse aluminum foil. She’d rinse it in the sink and hang it over the faucet to dry. It always had these little crinkles in it so it never really wrapped right the second time around, especially on sandwiches. I took a turkey sandwich to school once wrapped in reused foil and the mayonnaise leaked everywhere. Mayonnaise is really hard to get out of a canvas backpack. I had to scrub it with baking soda for an hour. Baking soda is a miracle worker, really, you can use it for almost anything. You can even put an open box in the fridge to absorb smells. My husband put half an onion in the fridge without putting it in a container first, and the whole kitchen smelled like a deli for a week. I told him to put it in a Tupperware, but he never listens. But yes, my thoughts definitely do that, they just go and go and go.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being placed in your mind that aren't your own, or that events or messages in your environment are specifically directed at you, like something is meant just for you?\n\nPatient: Directed at me? Well, yes, exactly. I know the wound-care charts are showing a pattern that management absolutely does not want anyone to see. It's so obvious to me. My supervisor keeps trying to brush it off, telling me it's just late entries or coding mistakes. Coding mistakes! Please. I can tell they're watching what I write now because I'm the one who actually caught it. They think I don't notice them monitoring my computer. \n\nSpeaking of the computers, IT makes us change our passwords every thirty days, which is just exhausting. You have to use a capital letter, a number, and a special character, and you can't reuse the last ten passwords! My husband just writes his passwords on a yellow sticky note and sticks it right to his monitor at home. I tell him, \"Anyone can see that!\" but he says nobody goes in his home office anyway. Which is true, because his office is a complete disaster zone. He has stacks of old magazines from the 90s in there. *National Geographic*, mostly. It just seems so unfair to change the rules on Pluto after all this time. But yes, they are definitely watching what I write in those charts because they know I'm onto them.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever felt like thoughts are being placed in your mind that aren't your own, or like things happening around you—like something on TV, in the newspaper, or something someone said—were meant specifically for you, even if others didn't see it that way?\n\nPatient: I feel like you're asking me the exact same thing again, Doctor.
